# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.23372319715296233
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges
rag-031 · chunk300-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

To evaluate a prompt experiment without confusing retrieval and generation, you should separate the evaluation of the retrieval component from the generation component. For retrieval, use metrics like Recall@K and Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR) to assess how well the system retrieves relevant documents, independent of the generation quality. For generation, evaluate the final output using metrics such as faithfulness, relevance, and fluency, while holding the retrieval results fixed. This prevents conflating errors from retrieval with those from generation.

Citations: doc-05#chunk-001, doc-05#chunk-002

Judge: 5 — The candidate correctly identifies the need to separate retrieval and generation evaluation, specifies retrieval metrics (Recall@K, MRR) and generation metrics (faithfulness, relevance, fluency), and advises holding retrieval fixed to avoid confusion. This fully addresses the reference requirement.

Retrieved evidence

rag-031 · chunk300-overlap50-top4-evidence_first · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

To evaluate a prompt experiment without confusing retrieval and generation, you should isolate the two stages. For retrieval, use metrics like Recall@k and Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR) that measure how well the retriever finds relevant documents, independent of the prompt. For generation, fix the retrieved context and evaluate the prompt's ability to produce correct answers using metrics like faithfulness or answer accuracy. This separation ensures that improvements in one stage are not mistakenly attributed to the other.

Citations: doc-05#chunk-001, doc-05#chunk-002, doc-06#chunk-001

Judge: 5 — The candidate correctly identifies the need to isolate retrieval and generation, and specifies separate metrics for each stage, fully addressing the reference requirement.

# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Limitations

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.11694106924093721
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.1522773975253763
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

# Embeddings and Caching

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.3
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

## Example

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.25863030005593607
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.17707958056758874
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.279078152825719
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

Answer

For retrieval, use metrics like Recall@K and reciprocal rank, which measure whether expected documents are retrieved, independent of the generated answer. For generation, evaluate the final answer quality (e.g., faithfulness, correctness) given the retrieved context. This separation ensures that retrieval failures are not attributed to generation and vice versa.

# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.1632993161855452
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

# Embeddings and Caching

## Limitations

# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

rag-012 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

Reranking can improve precision when the initial retrieval returns a set of candidates that includes relevant documents but ranks them poorly, and the reranker is able to reorder them to place more relevant documents at the top. This is particularly effective when the retrieval stage is not perfectly aligned with the notion of relevance used for the final task, and the reranker can better capture the nuanced relevance. However, if the initial retrieval misses relevant documents entirely (i.e., Recall@K is zero), reranking cannot help because the relevant evidence is not in the candidate set.

Citations: doc-11#chunk-000

Judge: 5 — The candidate correctly states that reranking improves precision only when useful evidence is already present in the first-stage candidates, and explicitly notes that if relevant documents are missing (Recall@K=0), reranking cannot help. This fully matches the reference requirement.

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

## Example

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.25863030005593607
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.17707958056758874
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.

rag-031 · chunk600-overlap50-top2-evidence_first · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

To evaluate a prompt experiment without confusing retrieval and generation, you should isolate the two stages. For retrieval, use metrics like Recall@K and reciprocal rank, which measure whether the expected documents are retrieved, independent of the final answer. For generation, evaluate the quality of the answer given the retrieved context, using metrics like faithfulness or correctness. This separation ensures that improvements in one stage are not mistakenly attributed to the other.

Citations: doc-05#chunk-000

Judge: 5 — The candidate correctly identifies the need to separate retrieval and generation metrics, explicitly mentions Recall@K and reciprocal rank for retrieval, and faithfulness/correctness for generation, fully covering the reference requirement.

Retrieved evidence

# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

## Example

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead 
doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.1632993161855452
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.1522773975253763
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

rift, so live runs must record actual usage and pricing verification dates.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.03333333333333333
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

An embedding enables a system to compute semantic similarity between texts.

# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea

## Limitations

# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.056819098972713665
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

Answer

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model. It improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence. It cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

Judge: 5 — The candidate correctly and completely answers the question, covering the core definition and additional relevant details from the reference.

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.3162277660168379
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

rag-012 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-direct · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

Reranking can improve precision when the initial retrieval has high recall but low precision, i.e., when relevant documents are present in the retrieved set but ranked low among many irrelevant ones. This is typically the case when Recall@K is high but precision is low, and the failure is not generation-limited but retrieval-limited. Reranking reorders the retrieved documents to place more relevant ones higher, thereby improving precision at the top of the list.

Citations: doc-11#chunk-000

The answer is partially correct but has a material omission of the core reference requirement.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.08340576562282996
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-10#chunk-000 · score=0.0758643241810882
# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

doc-07#chunk-000 · score=0.25039154291806714
# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

## Definition

Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

## Example

## Limitations

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.3697609549990482
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead 
doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.3286335345030996
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea

# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

RAG does not guarantee correctness when the corpus is incomplete, retrieval misses evidence, o
doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.06201736729460423
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.25863030005593607
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.17707958056758874
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

rag-031 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-direct · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

To evaluate a prompt experiment without confusing retrieval and generation, use separate metrics for each stage. For retrieval, use Recall@K and reciprocal rank, which measure whether relevant documents are retrieved. For generation, use answer accuracy or faithfulness metrics. Additionally, isolate the effect of retrieval by fixing the prompt and varying retrieval parameters, or fix retrieval and vary the prompt. This prevents conflating retrieval failures with generation failures.

Citations: doc-05#chunk-000, doc-01#chunk-000

Judge: 5 — The candidate correctly identifies the need to use separate metrics for retrieval and generation, directly addressing the reference requirement. It mentions specific retrieval metrics (Recall@K, reciprocal rank) and generation metrics (accuracy, faithfulness), and suggests isolating variables to avoid confusion. This fully covers the core reference requirement without omissions.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.2309401076758503
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.18602290120845702
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead 
doc-10#chunk-000 · score=0.17992804317122027
# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.

## Trade-offs

## Limitations

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.14108316199577509
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.
doc-02#chunk-000 · score=0.12751534261266764
# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

Answer

An experiment should connect performance reporting with failure diagnosis by first committing the experiment record (including commit SHA, dataset hash, prompt hash, configuration, model IDs, and random seed) to a SQLite database in WAL mode, then writing case results, and finally generating static JSON and HTML reports from that committed record. This ensures that reports are traceable to the exact experimental conditions, enabling diagnosis of failures by correlating performance metrics with the recorded metadata.

# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.

# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.17817416127494956
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

# Embeddings and Caching

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.

# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.1522773975253763
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

rift, so live runs must record actual usage and pricing verification dates.

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.03333333333333333
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Limitations

doc-02#chunk-000 · score=0.11405318970244022
# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.
rag-010 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-evidence_first · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

Recall@K measures whether the expected evidence is present in the top K retrieved passages. It is a retrieval metric that indicates if the relevant document is retrieved within the top K results, with a value of 1 if present and 0 otherwise.

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.056819098972713665
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead
rag-011 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-evidence_first · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model. It improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence. Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply but add one scoring operation per candidate. A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

Citations: doc-06#chunk-000

Judge: 5 — The candidate correctly and completely answers the question, covering the core definition and all key aspects from the reference, including the purpose, condition for improvement, trade-off, and limitation.

# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.08340576562282996
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-10#chunk-000 · score=0.0758643241810882
# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

doc-07#chunk-000 · score=0.25039154291806714
# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.2078698548207745
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

cannot compensate for a provider that silently changes a model behind the same identifier.

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.3697609549990482
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead 
doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.3286335345030996
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

A reproducible run records the commit SHA, dataset hash, prompt hash, configuration, model IDs, and random seed.

## Trade-offs

Local SQLite keeps deployment simple, but it is not a substitute for a distributed high-write database.

## Example

The runner writes case results first, then generates static JSON and HTML reports from the committed experiment record.

## Limitations

# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea

# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

RAG does not guarantee correctness when the corpus is incomplete, retrieval misses evidence, o
doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.06201736729460423
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

rag-026 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-evidence_first · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

The two mechanisms are chunk overlap and text hashing. Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable. Text hashing, specifically the normalized text hash, is used in embedding cache keys to detect changed sources.

Citations: doc-03#chunk-000, doc-04#chunk-000

Judge: 4 — The candidate correctly identifies both mechanisms: chunk overlap and text hashing. It explains chunk overlap's role in preserving boundary facts and mentions the normalized text hash in embedding cache keys for detecting changed sources. The only minor omission is not explicitly stating 'content hashes' as the term for detecting changed sources, but the description is accurate and covers the core requirements.

doc-03#chunk-000 · score=0.25863030005593607
# Text Chunking

ID: doc-03

## Definition

Chunking divides a normalized document into retrieval units that fit the embedding and prompt limits.
Chunk overlap repeats boundary text so facts split near an edge can remain recoverable.

## Trade-offs

Small chunks improve targeting but lose context, while large chunks preserve context but may dilute similarity scores.

## Example

A 400-character chunk with 50-character overlap advances by 350 characters after each split.

## Limitations

One chunk size cannot be assumed optimal for every document shape, language, or question type.

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.17707958056758874
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.

rag-027 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-evidence_first · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

Chunking and cache identity work together by ensuring that cached embeddings are reused only when the chunk's text hash and the embedding model identifier match, as described in doc-04. This means that before retrieval, the system checks the cache using a key that includes the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version. If a chunk's text or the model changes, the cache identity changes, preventing stale embeddings from being used.

Citations: doc-04#chunk-000

Judge: 4 — The candidate correctly explains that cache keys bind vectors to text and model identity, covering the core reference requirement. It mentions text hash and model identifier, and notes preprocessing version, which is a minor addition. It omits explicit mention of 'preprocessing' as a separate identity component in the key, but the core idea is fully addressed. The answer is faithful to the evidence and answers the question, with only a minor omission of explicitly naming 'preprocessing' as part of the identity.

# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

rag-031 · chunk600-overlap50-top4-evidence_first · deepseek-v4-flash

Answer

To evaluate a prompt experiment without confusing retrieval and generation, isolate the two stages. For retrieval, use metrics like Recall@K and reciprocal rank on the retrieved chunks, independent of the final answer. For generation, fix the retrieved context and evaluate the answer quality separately. This separation prevents conflating retrieval failures with generation failures.

Citations: doc-05#chunk-000, doc-01#chunk-000

doc-05#chunk-000 · score=0.2309401076758503
# Dense Retrieval

ID: doc-05

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.18602290120845702
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead 
doc-10#chunk-000 · score=0.17992804317122027
# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr
doc-09#chunk-000 · score=0.12751534261266764
# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

## Trade-offs

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea

# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

## Limitations

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.14108316199577509
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.
doc-02#chunk-000 · score=0.12751534261266764
# Document Ingestion

ID: doc-02

## Definition

Ingestion converts source files into normalized documents with stable IDs and metadata.
A content hash detects changes without depending on file modification times.

## Trade-offs

Strict normalization improves reproducibility, but aggressive cleanup can remove tables, headings, or other useful structure.

## Example

An ingestion job stores the source path, document ID, title, and SHA-256 content hash for every Markdown file.

## Limitations

Parsing rules are format-specific, and scanned documents require a separate OCR step.

# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

## Definition

Dense retrieval ranks chunks by cosine similarity between query and chunk embeddings.
Recall@K measures whether at least one expected document appears in the first K retrieval results.

## Trade-offs

Increasing top K can improve recall, but it consumes prompt tokens and may introduce distracting evidence.

## Example

If an expected document is ranked third, Recall@3 is one and reciprocal rank is one third.

## Limitations

Dense retrieval can miss exact identifiers and rare terms that lexical retrieval handles well.

doc-09#chunk-000 · score=0.150278272444572
# Abstention Evaluation

ID: doc-09

## Definition

Abstention means explicitly declining to answer when the controlled corpus lacks sufficient evidence.
False-answer rate is the fraction of unanswerable cases that receive a substantive answer.

## Trade-offs

Aggressive abstention reduces hallucinations but can reject answerable questions, so over-abstention must also be measured.

## Example

For an unsupported pricing question, the system returns an empty citation list and sets abstained to true.

## Limitations

String matching alone cannot reliably distinguish a cautious answer from a rea

doc-06#chunk-000 · score=0.2078698548207745
# Candidate Reranking

ID: doc-06

## Definition

A reranker reorders a small candidate set using a query-document relevance model.
Reranking improves precision only when the first-stage retriever already includes useful evidence.

## Trade-offs

Cross-encoder rerankers inspect query-document pairs more deeply, but add one scoring operation per candidate.

## Example

A retriever returns 20 chunks, a reranker scores those 20, and the prompt receives the best 4.

## Limitations

A reranker cannot recover a relevant document that was absent from the candidate set.

doc-12#chunk-000 · score=0.1597191412499851
# Reproducible Deployment

ID: doc-12

## Definition

A reproducible run records the commit SHA, dataset hash, prompt hash, configuration, model IDs, and random seed.
SQLite WAL mode lets readers continue while a coordinator serializes experiment writes.

## Trade-offs

Local SQLite keeps deployment simple, but it is not a substitute for a distributed high-write database.

## Example

The runner writes case results first, then generates static JSON and HTML reports from the committed experiment record.

## Limitations

Reproducibility metadata cannot compensate for a provider that silently cha

doc-04#chunk-000 · score=0.2995041904296595
# Embeddings and Caching

ID: doc-04

## Definition

An embedding maps text to a numeric vector so semantic similarity can be computed.
Embedding caches must include the model name, normalized text hash, and preprocessing version in the key.

## Trade-offs

Larger embedding models may improve semantic separation, but they increase latency, storage, and migration cost.

## Example

The vector for a cached chunk is reused only when both its text hash and embedding-model identifier match.

## Limitations

Cosine similarity reflects vector geometry, not factual correctness or permission to answer.
doc-07#chunk-000 · score=0.23973165074269204
# Grounded Prompting

ID: doc-07

## Definition

A grounded prompt tells the model to answer only from supplied evidence and to cite stable document IDs.
The evidence-first variant asks the model to list supporting facts before composing the final answer.

## Trade-offs

More instructions can improve format adherence, but they consume context and may conflict with one another.

## Example

The response contract contains an answer string, a citation list, and an abstained boolean.

## Limitations

Prompt instructions reduce unsupported answers but do not provide a hard factual guarantee.

# Cost and Latency Controls

ID: doc-10

## Definition

A conservative preflight estimate multiplies bounded token counts by verified prices and a safety multiplier.
Latency reports should include mean, P50, and P95 rather than only an average.

## Trade-offs

Parallel requests reduce wall-clock time but increase rate-limit pressure and complicate ordered persistence.

## Example

A runner refuses to start when its buffered estimate exceeds ninety percent of the configured hard budget.

## Limitations

Token estimates and published prices can drift, so live runs must record actual usage and pr

doc-08#chunk-000 · score=0.14617633655117152
# RAG Evaluation

ID: doc-08

## Definition

Retrieval metrics and generation metrics must be reported separately to localize failures.
MRR rewards systems that place the first relevant result near the top of the ranking.

## Trade-offs

Reference metrics are repeatable and cheap, while model judges cover nuance but introduce cost and evaluator bias.

## Example

A run can have high Recall@5 and low answer correctness, showing that generation failed despite available evidence.

## Limitations

Aggregate scores hide case-level regressions unless reports preserve individual outcomes.

doc-11#chunk-000 · score=0.3127036494188127
# Failure Taxonomy

ID: doc-11

## Definition

A retrieval miss occurs when expected evidence is absent from the retrieved context.
A generation failure occurs when evidence is present but the answer is incorrect, unsupported, or malformed.

## Trade-offs

Fine-grained labels improve diagnosis, but annotation takes longer and ambiguous cases need adjudication.

## Example

When Recall@K is zero, the case is retrieval-limited; when Recall@K is one and correctness fails, it is generation-limited.

## Limitations

Some failures have multiple causes, so a taxonomy should preserve evidence instead

# RAG System Overview

ID: doc-01

## Definition

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) retrieves external evidence before an LLM writes an answer.
A RAG request normally includes ingestion, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and evaluation.

## Trade-offs

RAG can update knowledge without retraining the generator, but it adds retrieval latency and another source of failure.

## Example

A support assistant retrieves two product-policy passages and cites them in its response.

## Limitations

RAG does not guarantee correctness when the corpus is incomplete, retrieval misses evidence, o
